Just as foundation models at the molecular scale have succeeded in predicting biological traits such as molecular structure and function (<a id="xref-ref-12-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-12">Brixi et al., 2025</a>; <a id="xref-ref-76-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-76">Nguyen et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-43-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-43">Hayes et al., 2025</a>; <a id="xref-ref-62-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-62">Lin et al., 2023</a>), models at the cellular scale hold promise for uncovering mechanisms underlying disease progression and advancing the discovery of more precise treatments. However, translating these successes to the cellular level has proven substantially more challenging. Single-cell foundation models have yet to achieve the same level of predictive accuracy or generalizability, largely due to the inherent complexity of cells as dynamic, context-dependent systems. Unlike the relatively static and homogeneous nature of individual DNA, RNA, or protein molecules, cellular systems exhibit substantial heterogeneity and are deeply influenced by environmental and temporal factors. Two central challenges for modeling cellular systems are the intrinsic heterogeneity of cell populations undergoing perturbation which cannot be fully resolved using known covariates, and the variability introduced across different experiments and datasets.</p><p id="p-53">Our model, S<span class="sc">tate</span>, presents a scalable approach for learning a foundation model of cell state and behavior across diverse cellular contexts and experimental conditions. The State Transition model (ST) uniquely learns perturbation effects across cell populations while still maintaining single-cell resolution in its predictions, thereby capturing residual heterogeneity not explained by known experimental or biological covariates. While past work has tackled heterogeneity in single-cell data by modeling cells as groups (<a id="xref-ref-84-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-84">Persad et al., 2023</a>; <a id="xref-ref-9-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-9">Boyeau et al., 2022</a>; <a id="xref-ref-25-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-25">Dann et al., 2022</a>), S<span class="sc">tate</span> uniquely leverages a permutation-invariant transformer architecture (<a id="xref-ref-59-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-59">Lee et al., 2019</a>; <a id="xref-ref-117-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-117">Zaheer et al., 2017</a>) to directly model perturbation effects across unpaired distributions of cells. This frees S<span class="sc">tate</span> from predefined assumptions on distributional structure, unlike approaches based on optimal transport (<a id="xref-ref-13-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-13">Bunne et al., 2023</a>; <a id="xref-ref-19-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-19">Chen et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-29-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-29">Demir et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-32-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-32">Dong et al., 2023</a>). Moreover, our theoretical findings establish that, in the asymptotic limit and under mild regularity conditions, the unique continuous OT map (defined by a fixed quadratic cost) linking unperturbed and perturbed cell populations is provably contained within our model’s solution space (<a id="xref-statement-2-1" class="xref-statement" href="#statement-2">Theorem 2</a>). This means S<span class="sc">tate</span> may learn the OT map when necessary, without explicitly constraining itself to a rigid, predefined OT formulation or requiring specialized architectural components typically associated with explicit OT solvers. Furthermore, by introducing explicit Jacobian-based constraints, we show that S<span class="sc">tate</span> can uniquely identify and learn this continuous optimal transport map (<a id="xref-statement-3-1" class="xref-statement" href="#statement-3">Theorem 3</a>), positioning it as a powerful, theoretically grounded Neural OT solver.</p><p id="p-54">The central goal for S<span class="sc">tate</span> is to reliably simulate single-cell perturbational experiments across diverse cell contexts. We used this objective to guide the design of a comprehensive model evaluation suite, Cell-Eval, focusing on performance along three key dimensions: gene expression counts, differential expression, perturbation effect sizes. To our knowledge, S<span class="sc">tate</span> is the first machine learning model to consistently outperform simple baselines such as the mean or linear models on the cell context generalization task, across almost all metrics and on multiple datasets. Moreover, the inclusion of embeddings from our cell embedding model, SE, enables more effective zero-shot perturbation effect prediction in novel cell contexts, a core goal for developing truly generalizable virtual cells. While S<span class="sc">tate</span> shows strong zero-shot performance when predicting perturbation effect sizes, metrics that reflect subtler changes, such as the accuracy of individual DE genes, are more sensitive to dataset size and quality. With the growth of large-scale perturbational atlases (<a id="xref-ref-93-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-93">Rood et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-48-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-48">Huang et al., 2025a</a>; <a id="xref-ref-120-9" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-120">Zhang et al., 2025</a>; <a id="xref-ref-94-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-94">Rood et al., 2025</a>), we expect performance on these subtler metrics to improve, and S<span class="sc">tate</span> is well-positioned to leverage these datasets given its improved robustness to cellular and data variability. Furthermore, we have found that ST attention maps demonstrate sensitivity to cell set heterogeneity, highlighting interpretability as a promising avenue for further understanding gene regulatory mechanisms using S<span class="sc">tate</span> (<strong><a id="xref-sec-72-1" class="xref-sec" href="#sec-72">Section 6</a></strong>).</p><p id="p-55">When applied to previously seen contexts, we have found S<span class="sc">tate</span>’s predictions are biologically meaningful, accurately capturing context-specific perturbation effects, such as cell-type specific DE genes. Predicted post-perturbation profiles were also found to capture non-transcriptional cellular phenotypes such as cell survival. These capabilities position S<span class="sc">tate</span> as a step towards realizing the broader vision of a virtual cell–a model capable of exploring the space of possible cell states (<a id="xref-ref-14-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-14">Bunne et al., 2024a</a>; <a id="xref-ref-78-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-78">Noutahi et al., 2025</a>) and guiding autonomous systems, such as AI agents, for experimental design (<a id="xref-ref-96-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-96">Roohani et al., 2024b</a>; <a id="xref-ref-49-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-49">Huang et al., 2025b</a>; <a id="xref-ref-91-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-91">Rizvi et al., 2025</a>; <a id="xref-ref-112-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-112">Wu et al., 2025</a>). S<span class="sc">tate</span> contributes to this vision by advancing the state-of-the-art in perturbation-response modeling and by introducing an architecture that can meaningfully leverage the growing size and diversity of single-cell perturbation datasets. Its flexible transformer architecture can support a range of tasks that involve modeling transitions between distinct populations such as differentiation, development, and reprogramming. S<span class="sc">tate</span> is also agnostic to the choice of cell representation, which enables seamless extension to additional data modalities, including proteomics and morphological measurements, as well as literature-based knowledge. By incorporating mechanisms for perturbation featurization withing S<span class="sc">tate</span>, we expect to further lower the barriers between different modalities such as drugs and gene knockdowns, while also opening the door to combinatorial perturbation prediction. Although S<span class="sc">tate</span> has been trained and evaluated on over 70 cell contexts, we have not tested its usability on entirely held-out datasets for which no cell contexts have been seen during training. Further improvements in SE through training on larger datasets, such as scBaseCount (<a id="xref-ref-115-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-115">Youngblut et al., 2025</a>), will be instrumental in generalizing predictions across such diverse settings.</p></div><div class="section" id="sec-8"><h2 class="">4. Methods</h2><div id="sec-9" class="subsection"><h3>4.1. Data Generation Process</h3><p id="p-56">We assume that the observed log-normalized perturbed expression state of each cell, <em>X</em><sub>p</sub>, is a random variable generated from an <em>unobservable</em> unperturbed state, <em>X</em><sub>0</sub>, which itself is a random variable representing the cell’s underlying expression state. <em>X</em><sub>0</sub> is drawn from a basal cell distribution 𝒟<sub>basal</sub> for a given set of covariates (e.g., cell line, batch condition, etc), and the perturbation effect can be modeled as follows:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-2"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-6.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-6.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where</p><ul class="list-unord " id="list-2"><li id="list-item-5"><p id="p-57"><em>T</em><sub><em>p</em></sub>(<em>X</em><sub>0</sub>): True effect caused by perturbation <em>p</em>.</p></li><li id="list-item-6"><p id="p-58"><em>ε</em>: Experiment-specific technical noise, assumed independent of <em>X</em><sub>0</sub>.</p></li></ul><p id="p-59">As single-cell transcriptomic measurements destroy the cell, <em>X</em><sub>0</sub> is unobservable, so directly modeling <strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-2-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-2">Eq. 2</a></strong> is not feasible. Instead, our method operates on the observable 𝒟<sub>basal</sub> to predict the perturbed state, denoted as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-4"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-4.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-4.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. This forms the basis of our model:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-3"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-7.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-7.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p><p id="p-60">In this approximation, the true effect of the perturbation <em>T</em><sub><em>p</em></sub>(<em>X</em><sub>0</sub>) is now considered in the context of the entire basal population, denoted as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-5"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-5.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-5.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. Additionally, we explicitly introduce <em>H</em>(𝒟<sub>basal</sub>) to represent the biological heterogeneity inherent in the baseline population. This heterogeneity was implicitly removed when sampling <em>X</em><sub>0</sub> <em>~</em> 𝒟<sub>basal</sub> in the first equation but is made explicit here to reflect our shift to a distributional view. <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-6"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-6.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-6.gif"/></noscript></span></span> can be seen as a distributional analogue of <em>X</em><sub>p</sub>, allowing us to model the perturbed state based on observable population characteristics rather than unobservable individual cell states.</p><p id="p-61"><strong><a id="xref-fig-1-6" class="xref-fig" href="#F1">Fig. 1A</a></strong> illustrates these assumptions. S<span class="sc">tate</span> is designed to reflect the assumptions of this data generation process: it directly models <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-7"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-7.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-7.gif"/></noscript></span></span> with S<span class="sc">tate</span> Transition (ST) and accounts for <em>ε</em> with State Embedding (SE).</p></div><div id="sec-10" class="subsection"><h3>4.2. Task Description</h3><p id="p-62">We now describe the core prediction tasks used for evaluation.</p><div id="sec-11" class="subsection"><h4>4.2.1. Underrepresented Context Generalization Task</h4><p id="p-63">To evaluate the generalization capabilities of perturbation models, we first test the generalization of perturbation effects within Perturb-Seq datasets (<strong><a id="xref-fig-2-15" class="xref-fig" href="#F2">Fig. 2</a></strong>). Given the dataset of interest, let the collection of biological contexts (cell types or cell lines in some dataset) be 𝒞 and the perturbation catalogue be 𝒫. For every context <em>c ∈</em> 𝒞, we write 𝒫<sub><em>c</em></sub> <em>⊆</em> 𝒫 for the subset of perturbations that were profiled in that context, and we denote by <em>X</em><sub><em>p,c</em></sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>d</em></sup> the gene–expression vector generated for the pair (<em>p, c</em>) according to <strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-2-2" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-2">Eq. 2</a></strong>. To test the generalization capabilities of the model in an underrepresented context, we fix a test context <em>c</em><sup>*</sup> <em>∈</em> 𝒞. We then choose a proportion <em>α</em> = 0.30 and draw a support set
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-4"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-8.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-8.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
uniformly at random. The remaining perturbations form the target perturbation set <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-8"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-8.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-8.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. The training and test splits are then given as follows:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-5"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-9.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-9.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-6"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-10.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-10.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Thus, the model sees all perturbations in 𝒞\{<em>c</em><sup>*</sup>} and only 30% of the perturbations in the test context <em>c</em><sup>*</sup>. If |𝒞 | is large, a diverse fixed subset 𝒞<sub>test</sub> <em>⊂</em> 𝒞 of <em>n</em> contexts is designated as test contexts. We form 𝒟<sub>train</sub> from the 𝒞\𝒞<sub>test</sub> contexts, as well as 30% of the perturbations from each <em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub> <em>∈</em> 𝒞<sub>test</sub>. We then construct multiple 𝒟<sub>test</sub>, one from each <em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub> <em>∈</em> 𝒞<sub>test</sub>, each containing the remaining unseen perturbations from <em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub>. If |𝒞 | is small, we <em>iteratively</em> leave out a single context <em>c</em><sup>*</sup> = 𝒞\{<em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub>}, form 𝒟<sub>train</sub> with the remaining <em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub> contexts as well as 30% of the perturbations from <em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub> and 𝒟<sub>test</sub> from the remaining unseen perturbations from <em>c</em><sub><em>j</em></sub>. This iteration creates multiple training sets, allowing to report performance over separately trained models. In both cases, we report the mean loss across 𝒟<sub>test</sub>, yielding a robust estimate of the model’s ability to extrapolate to unseen perturbations within an underrepresented context. Unperturbed cells from all contexts including the underrepresented context are available to the model during training.</p></div><div id="sec-12" class="subsection"><h4>4.2.2. Zero-Shot Context Generalization Task</h4><p id="p-64">Next, we evaluate the ability to generalize perturbation effects in a zero-shot manner across biological contexts (<strong><a id="xref-fig-3-9" class="xref-fig" href="#F3">Fig. 3F</a></strong>). To enhance performance on this task, we include a pre-training step. Each model is initially pre-trained on one or more large perturbation datasets (e.g., Tahoe-100M) and subsequently fine-tuned to a different dataset, referred to as the query dataset, via full fine-tuning. The model is then tested on a held-out context within the query dataset.</p><p id="p-65">Specifically, the model is first pre-trained on one or more datasets that include perturbations across multiple contexts. Then, given a query dataset, we hold out one context and use the remaining contexts for fine-tuning. The contexts used for fine-tuning contain <em>a superset of the perturbations</em> found in the held-out context. The fine-tuned model is evaluated on its ability to predict the effects of those perturbations within the held-out context of the query dataset. Formally, the fine-tuning process involves partitioning the set of contexts in the query dataset into 𝒞<sub>fine-tune</sub> <em>⊂</em> 𝒞 and 𝒞<sub>test</sub> <em>⊂</em> 𝒞 with 𝒞 <sub>fine-tune</sub> <em>∩</em> 𝒞<sub>test</sub> = ∅. The data used in the fine-tuning phase
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-7"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-11.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-11.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
contain all perturbations in 𝒞<sub>fine-tune</sub>. After fine-tuning <em>f</em><sub><em>θ</em></sub> on 𝒟<sub>fine-tune</sub>, we evaluate it on the same perturbations <em>p ∈</em> 𝒫 but in unseen, held-out cell lines <em>c ∈</em> 𝒞<sub>test</sub>. Thus, the test set is
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-8"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-12.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-12.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This task directly assesses whether the model has learned generalizable relationships between biological context and perturbation response. Unperturbed cells from all contexts including the held out context are available to the model during training.</p></div></div><div id="sec-13" class="subsection"><h3>4.3. State Transition Model (ST)</h3><p id="p-66">ST is a deep learning model that learns the transcriptomic responses to perturbations across populations of cells. The model uses a transformer architecture with self-attention across cells to predict perturbation effects between control and perturbed cell distributions. Let
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-9"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-13.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-13.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
denote a dataset of single–cell RNA-sequencing measurements over the gene set, with <em>G</em> the number of genes. Here, <strong>x</strong><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> represents the normalized expression vector for cell <em>i</em>, after its raw count values have been processed. Specifically, the raw count value observed for gene <em>j</em> in cell <em>i</em> after perturbation <em>p</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> (if any) is initially <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-9"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-9.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-9.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. These raw counts are depth-normalized and log-transformed using Scanpy(<kbd>normalize_total <em>→</em> log1p</kbd>), yielding non-negative, real-valued expression vectors <strong>x</strong><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup>. Each cell is annotated with perturbation label <em>p</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> <em>∈</em> {1, …, <em>P</em>} <em>∪</em> {<kbd>ctrl</kbd>}, a biological context or cell line label <em>ℓ</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> <em>∈</em> {1, …, <em>L</em>}, and an optional batch effect label <em>b</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> <em>∈</em> {1, …, <em>B</em>}.</p><div id="sec-14" class="subsection"><h4>4.3.1. Formation of Cell Sets</h4><p id="p-67">We group cells into sets based on their biological context, perturbation, and batch labels:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-10"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-14.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-14.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Let <em>N</em><sub><em>ℓ,p,b</em></sub> = |𝒞<sub><em>ℓ,p,b</em></sub>| denote the number of cells in each group. Given a target set size <em>S</em>, we partition 𝒞<sub><em>ℓ,p,b</em></sub> into non-overlapping subsets of size <em>S</em> to construct a collection of tensors <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-10"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-10.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-10.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-11"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-11.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-11.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. If <em>N</em><sub><em>ℓ,p,b</em></sub> is not divisible by <em>S</em>, the remaining cells form a final, smaller set, which is padded to size <em>S</em> by sampling additional cells with replacement from itself.</p></div><div id="sec-15" class="subsection"><h4>4.3.2. Training on Cell Sets</h4><p id="p-68">During training, each set <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-12"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-12.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-12.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, where <em>p ∈</em> {1, …, <em>P</em>} {<kbd>ctrl</kbd>}, is paired with a corresponding control set. The control set is constructed by randomly sampling <em>S</em> control cells from the same cell line <em>ℓ</em>, and optionally same batch <em>b</em>. Formally, for each training example <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-13"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-13.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-13.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, we construct a paired control set via a <kbd>map</kbd> operation:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-11"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-15.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-15.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This mapping is applied uniformly, regardless of whether <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-14"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-14.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-14.gif"/></noscript></span></span> contains perturbed or control cells. The choice of <kbd>map</kbd> function effectively determines which sources of variation are explicitly controlled for. By conditioning on specific covariates (e.g., cell line <em>ℓ</em>, batch <em>b</em>), the mapping function reduces known sources of heterogeneity that could otherwise confound true perturbation signals. Different mapping strategies introduce different priors on non-perturbational variation. For instance, sampling control cells from the same batch can reduce technical variation but limit the number of unique cells per set, which may limit model performance.</p><p id="p-69">To construct mini-batches, we collect <em>B</em> such set pairs, <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-15"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-15.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-15.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, where different pairs may originate from a different combination of cell line, perturbation, and (optionally) batch. These are then arranged into the following tensors:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-12"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-16.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-16.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <em>D</em><sub>pert</sub> and <em>D</em><sub>batch</sub> denote the dimensionalities of the perturbation and batch embeddings, respectively. For S<span class="sc">tate</span>, we use one-hot encodings of perturbation and batch, so <em>D</em><sub>pert</sub> equals the number of unique perturbations across the data, and <em>D</em><sub>batch</sub> equals the number of unique batch labels. ST takes <strong>X</strong><sub>ctrl</sub> as input along with the perturbation embeddings <strong>Z</strong><sub>pert</sub> and learns to predict <strong>X</strong><sub>target</sub> as output, learning to transform control cell populations into their corresponding perturbed states.</p></div><div id="sec-16" class="subsection"><h4>4.3.3. Neural Network Modules</h4><p id="p-70">ST uses specialized encoders that map cellular expression profiles, perturbation labels, and optionally batch labels into a shared hidden dimension <em>d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>, which serves as the input to the transformer.</p><div id="sec-17" class="subsection"><h5>Control Cell Encoder</h5><p id="p-71">Each log-normalized expression vector <strong>x</strong><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>G</em></sup> is mapped to an embedding via a 4-layer MLP with GELU activations. The MLP <em>f</em><sub>cell</sub> is applied to each cell independently across the entire control tensor:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-13"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-17.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-17.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This transforms the input shape from (<em>B × S × G</em>) <em>→</em> (<em>B × S × d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>).</p></div><div id="sec-18" class="subsection"><h5>Perturbation Encoder</h5><p id="p-72">Perturbation labels are encoded into the same embedding dimension <em>d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>. For one-hot encoded perturbations, the input vector is passed through a 4-layer MLP with GELU activations:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-14"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-18.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-18.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This transforms the input shape (<em>B × S × D</em><sub>pert</sub>) <em>→</em> (<em>B × S × d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>) (note the perturbation embedding is the same for all cells within the same set of a given batch). Alternatively, when perturbations are represented by continuous features (e.g., molecular descriptors or gene embeddings), the embeddings are directly used in <strong>H</strong><sub>pert</sub> and we set <em>d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub> = <em>D</em><sub>pert</sub>.</p></div><div id="sec-19" class="subsection"><h5>Batch Encoder</h5><p id="p-73">To account for technical batch effects, batch labels <em>b</em><sub><em>i</em></sub> <em>∈</em> {1, …, <em>B</em>} are encoded into embeddings of dimension <em>d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-15"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-19.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-19.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <em>f</em><sub>batch</sub> is an embedding layer. This transforms the input shape from (<em>B × S × D</em><sub>batch</sub>) <em>→</em> (<em>B × S × d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>).</p></div><div id="sec-20" class="subsection"><h5>Transformer Inputs and Outputs</h5><p id="p-74">The final input to ST is constructed by summing the control cell embeddings with the perturbation and batch embeddings:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-16"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-20.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-20.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This composite representation is passed to the transformer backbone <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub> to model perturbation effects across the cell set. The output is computed as:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-17"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-21.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-21.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-16"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-16.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-16.gif"/></noscript></span></span> represents the final output. This formulation encourages the transformer <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub> to learn perturbation effects as residuals to the input representation <strong>H</strong>.</p></div><div id="sec-21" class="subsection"><h5>Gene Reconstruction Head</h5><p id="p-75">When working with inputs directly in expression space, the gene reconstruction head maps the output of the transformer, <strong>O</strong>, back to gene expression space. This is done with a linear projection layer, applied independently to the <em>d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>-dimensional hidden representation of each token within each cell set. Specifically, for each cell <em>b</em> in the batch, where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-17"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-17.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-17.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is the transformer output for cell <em>b</em>, the reconstructed gene expression <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-18"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-18.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-18.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is given by:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-18"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-22.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-22.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-19"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-19.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-19.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and <strong>b</strong><sub>recon</sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>G</em></sup> are learnable parameters. This operation transforms the hidden representations, yielding reconstructed log-transformed gene expression values for each cell in the batch.</p></div></div><div id="sec-22" class="subsection"><h4>4.3.4. Learning Perturbation Effects with Maximum Mean Discrepancy</h4><p id="p-76">ST is trained to minimize the discrepancy between predicted <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-20"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-20.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-20.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and observed (<strong>X</strong><sub>target</sub>) transcriptomic responses. This is quantified using the Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) (<a id="xref-ref-39-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-39">Gretton et al., 2012</a>), a statistical measure of distance between two probability distributions based on their embeddings in a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS), via a kernel function of choice. MMD has been applied previously to model single-cell perturbation effects (<a id="xref-ref-119-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-119">Zhang et al., 2023</a>).</p><p id="p-77">For each mini-batch element <em>b</em>, we consider the set of <em>S</em> predicted cell expression vectors of cells within the batch (therefore those sharing the same combination of cell line <em>ℓ</em> and perturbation <em>p</em>, as described in <strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-12-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-12">Eq. 12</a></strong>). With a slight abuse of notation, we denote this set as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-21"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-21.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-21.gif"/></noscript></span></span> such that <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-22"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-22.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-22.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, and the corresponding set of <em>S</em> target cell expression vectors of cells within the batch as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-23"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-23.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-23.gif"/></noscript></span></span> such that <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-24"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-24.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-24.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. The MMD is then used to minimize the distance between the empirical probability distributions implicitly defined by these two finite sets of vectors.</p><p id="p-78">The squared MMD between the predicted and observed cell sets is computed as:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-19"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-23.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-23.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <em>k</em>(<em>·,·</em>) denotes the kernel function. The three terms correspond to: (1) similarity within the predicted set, (2) similarity within the observed set, and (3) cross-similarity between predicted and observed sets.</p><p id="p-79">We use the energy distance kernel:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-20"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-24.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-24.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
implemented via the geomloss library (<a id="xref-ref-36-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-36">Feydy et al., 2019</a>).</p><p id="p-80">For notational convenience, for a training minibatch of <em>B</em> cell sets, we define the batch-averaged MMD loss as the average of these MMD<sup>2</sup> values:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-21"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-25.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-25.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Minimizing this loss encourages the model to generate sets of perturbed cell expression vectors whose overall statistical properties, as captured by the MMD, consistent with perturbation labels and match those of the observed cell sets. The total loss is:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-22"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-26.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-26.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-23" class="subsection"><h4>4.3.5. Training ST in Embedding Spaces</h4><p id="p-81">ST offers the flexibility to be trained either directly in gene expression space or in a specified embedding space. When trained directly in gene expression space, ST operates on the top 2,000 highly variable genes (HVGs) as identified in our preprocessing. In this mode, the model is referred to as ST+HVG in our results. To enable training in an embedding space, the architecture is modified to include an additional expression decoder. Formally, let <em>E</em> denote the dimensionality of the embedding space, where typically <em>E ≪ G</em>. The tensors <strong>X</strong><sub>target</sub> and <strong>X</strong><sub>ctrl</sub>, now denoted as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-25"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-25.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-25.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-26"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-26.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-26.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, are of dimension <em>B × S × E</em>. Accordingly, <em>f</em><sub>cell</sub> is modified to transform the input shape from (<em>B × S × E</em>) <em>→</em> (<em>B × S × d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>), and <em>f</em><sub>recon</sub> is modified to transform the output shape from (<em>B × S × d</em><sub><em>h</em></sub>) <em>→</em> (<em>B × S × E</em>). This output is denoted as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-27"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-27.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-27.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. The other encoders and the transformer itself remain unchanged.</p><p id="p-82">To recover the original gene expression of the target cells, <strong>X</strong><sub>target</sub>, we train an additional decoder head <em>f</em><sub>decode</sub>. This is a multi-layer MLP with dropout that maps from the embedding space back to the full gene expression space:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-23"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-27.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-27.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This transforms the predicted embeddings from (<em>B × S × E</em>) <em>→</em> (<em>B × S × G</em>) to recover gene expression profiles.</p><p id="p-83">The ST loss is hence modified to make use of the two target sets: <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-28"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-28.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-28.gif"/></noscript></span></span> containing the target embeddings, and <strong>X</strong><sub>target</sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>B×S×G</em></sup> containing the target gene expression profiles. Specifically, the model is trained using a weighted combination of MMD losses in the embedding and gene expression spaces:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-24"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-28.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-28.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
The expression loss is down-weighted by a factor of 0.1 to balance the two terms and avoid overwhelming the primary objective in embedding space. This encourages the model to learn perturbation effects primarily in the embedding space while simultaneously decoding to expression space. Our experiments demonstrate that the embedding space improves learning of perturbational effects across datasets, suggesting that the smoother structure of the embedding space facilitates better modeling of perturbation biology.</p></div></div><div id="sec-24" class="subsection"><h3>4.4. State Embedding Model (SE)</h3><p id="p-84">ST motivates the development of high-quality cell embeddings that capture relevant biological signal while reducing technical artifacts. For this, we developed the State Embedding model (SE), a self-supervised model trained with a gene expression prediction objective to learn cell representations from single-cell RNA sequencing data. The embeddings produced by SE serve as inputs to ST, enabling more robust transfer across datasets and biological contexts.</p><div id="sec-25" class="subsection"><h4>4.4.1. Gene Representation via Protein Language Models</h4><p id="p-85">Our dataset (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-9-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-9">Eq. 9</a></strong>) consists of cell expression measurements <strong>x</strong><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup>, where each entry <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-29"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-29.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-29.gif"/></noscript></span></span> represents the normalized expression level of gene <em>j</em> in cell <em>i</em>. Beyond these expression values, the SE model incorporates rich information about genes themselves through pretrained protein language model embeddings <strong>g</strong><sub><em>j</em></sub>.</p><p id="p-86">Following recent work (<a id="xref-ref-97-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-97">Rosen et al., 2023</a>; <a id="xref-ref-82-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-82">Pearce et al., 2025</a>), we leverage pretrained protein language models to encode gene features. Gene embeddings are computed with ESM-2 (<kbd>esm2_t48_15B_UR50D</kbd> (<a id="xref-ref-62-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-62">Lin et al., 2023</a>)), first by computing transcript embeddings by averaging per-amino-acid embeddings for each protein-coding transcript in the gene, and then by averaging across all the transcripts in the gene. This featurization captures evolutionary and functional relationships between genes. Genes without Ensembl IDs are mapped using the MyGene API (<a id="xref-ref-111-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-111">Wu et al., 2013</a>) and Ensembl REST API (<a id="xref-ref-114-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-114">Yates et al., 2015</a>), with manual curation for ambiguous cases. Non-protein-coding genes are excluded from SE.</p><p id="p-87">Each gene embedding <strong>g</strong><sub><em>j</em></sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup>5120</sup> is projected into the model’s embedding dimension <em>h</em> via a learnable encoder:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-25"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-29.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-29.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <strong>W</strong><sub><em>g</em></sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup>5120×<em>h</em></sup> and <strong>b</strong><sub><em>g</em></sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>h</em></sup> are learnable parameters, and SiLU denotes the Sigmoid Linear Unit activation function (<a id="xref-ref-34-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-34">Elfwing et al., 2018</a>).</p></div><div id="sec-26" class="subsection"><h4>4.4.2. Cell Representation</h4><p id="p-88">We represent each cell <em>i</em> as a sequence of its most highly expressed genes in <strong>x</strong><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup>. For each cell, we construct an “expression set” by selecting the top <em>L</em> = 2048 genes ranked by log fold expression level. Empirically, increasing <em>L</em> beyond 2048 yielded diminishing returns in model performance. The expression set is then augmented with two special tokens:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-26"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-30.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-30.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <strong>z</strong><sub>cls</sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>h</em></sup> is a learnable classification token used to aggregate cell-level information, and <strong>z</strong><sub>ds</sub> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>h</em></sup> is a learnable dataset token that helps disentangle dataset-specific effects. Here, <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-30"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-30.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-30.gif"/></noscript></span></span> represents the projected embedding of the <em>ℓ</em>-th most highly expressed gene in cell <em>i</em>, with <em>ℓ ∈</em> {1 …, <em>L</em>}. The gene selection is cell-specific: different cells may have different sets of highly expressed genes, leading to different gene embeddings in their respective expression sets. If a cell expresses fewer than <em>L</em> genes, the expression set is padded to length <em>L</em> by randomly sampling from the pool of unexpressed genes. This maintains a fixed-length input for transformer processing.</p></div><div id="sec-27" class="subsection"><h4>4.4.3. Expression-Aware Embeddings</h4><p id="p-89">Although genes in the cell expression set are sorted by expression level, their magnitudes are not explicitly encoded or used by the model. Instead, SE incorporates expression values directly using an expression embedding scheme inspired by soft binning (<a id="xref-ref-41-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-41">Hao et al., 2024</a>). For the <em>ℓ</em>-th most expressed gene in cell <em>i</em>’s expression set (which corresponds to gene ID <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-31"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-31.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-31.gif"/></noscript></span></span>) with log-normalized expression value <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-32"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-32.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-32.gif"/></noscript></span></span> in cell <em>i</em>, we compute a soft bin assignment:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-27"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-31.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-31.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-28"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-32.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-32.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where MLP<sub>count</sub> : ℝ <em>→</em> ℝ<sup>10</sup> consists of two linear layers (dimensions 1 <em>→</em> 512 <em>→</em> 10) with LeakyReLU activation, and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-33"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-33.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-33.gif"/></noscript></span></span> are learnable bin embeddings of dimension <em>h</em>. The resulting expression encodings <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-34"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-34.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-34.gif"/></noscript></span></span> are added to the corresponding gene identity embeddings <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-35"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-35.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-35.gif"/></noscript></span></span>:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-29"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-33.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-33.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-28" class="subsection"><h4>4.4.4. Transformer Encoding</h4><p id="p-90">The input expression set, composed of expression-aware gene embeddings and special tokens, is passed through the transformer encoder <em>f</em><sub>SE</sub>:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-30"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-34.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-34.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where each <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-36"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-36.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-36.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is as in <strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-29-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-29">Eq. 27</a></strong>. Note that the expression encodings are set to zero for the special tokens <kbd>[CLS]</kbd> and <kbd>[DS]</kbd>.</p><p id="p-91">The output is a sequence of contextualized embeddings. The cell embedding is then extracted from the <kbd>[CLS]</kbd> token at position 0 and normalized:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-31"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-35.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-35.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This embedding serves as a summary representation of the cell’s transcriptomic state. Similarly, we extract the dataset representation from the <kbd>[DS]</kbd> token at position <em>L</em> + 1:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-32"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-36.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-36.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
This embedding is used to capture and account for dataset-specific effects during training.</p><p id="p-92">The final embedding is the concatenation of these two quantities:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-33"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-37.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-37.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-37"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-37.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-37.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is a learned linear projection of the dataset embedding from <em>h →</em> 10. This cell embedding <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-38"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-38.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-38.gif"/></noscript></span></span> serves as the input representation for individual cells in ST, enabling the multi-scale State architecture to leverage rich cellular representations for population-level perturbation modeling.</p></div><div id="sec-29" class="subsection"><h4>4.4.5. Pretraining Objectives</h4><p id="p-93">SE is trained using a self-supervised learning framework with two complementary objectives: (1) a gene expression prediction task, and (2) an auxiliary dataset classification task that helps disentangle technical batch effects from biological signal.</p><div id="sec-30" class="subsection"><h5>Gene Expression Prediction</h5><p id="p-94">During training, the model receives the complete input cell expression set (as described in <strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-26-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-26">Eq. 24</a></strong>), and is tasked with predicting expression values for a selected set of 1,280 genes per cell. To ensure coverage across the expression dynamic range, the target genes for prediction are drawn from three categories:</p><ul class="list-unord " id="list-3"><li id="list-item-7"><p id="p-95">𝒫 <sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup>, a set of |𝒫<sup>(i)</sup> | = 512 highly expressed genes (from the top <em>L</em> genes in the cell expression set <em>i</em>), 𝒩 <sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup>, a set of | 𝒩<sup>(i)</sup> |= 512 unexpressed genes (randomly sampled from genes not in the top <em>L</em> of cell <em>i</em>),</p></li><li id="list-item-8"><p id="p-96">ℛ a set of |𝒩<sup>(i)</sup> |= 256 genes randomly sampled from the full gene set, <em>shared for all cells in the batch</em>.</p></li></ul><p id="p-97">This results in 1,280 genes for which expression values must be predicted per cell. This strategy encourages the model to reconstruct expression values across a wide dynamic range and to learn meaningful representations of both expressed and silent genes, while having access to the complete transcriptomic context, through self attention, during prediction.</p></div><div id="sec-31" class="subsection"><h5>Expression Prediction Decoder</h5><p id="p-98">To learn from gene expression prediction across different datasets with varying read depth, we use an MLP decoder that combines multiple sources of information:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-34"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-38.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-38.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-39"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-39.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-39.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is the learned cell embedding (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-33-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-33">Eq. 31</a></strong>), <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-40"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-40.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-40.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is the embedding of the target gene (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-25-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-25">Eq. 23</a></strong>), and <em>r ∈</em> ℝ is a scalar read depth indicator, computed as the mean log expression of expressed genes in the input expression set for each cell <em>i</em>. These are concatenated and passed through MLP<sub>dec</sub>, which consists of two skip-connected blocks followed by a linear output layer that predicts the log expression for the target gene <em>j</em> in cell <em>i</em>.</p><p id="p-99">For each cell <em>i</em> in a training batch, let <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-41"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-41.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-41.gif"/></noscript></span></span> denote the row vector containing the set of predicted expression values for the genes in 𝒫 <sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> <em>∪</em> 𝒩 <sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> <em>∪</em> ℛ, and let <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-42"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-42.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-42.gif"/></noscript></span></span> denote the row vector with corresponding true expression values.
