class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table 3:</span> <span class="caption-title">Key model hyperparameters by dataset.</span><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div><div id="T4" class="table pos-float"><div class="table-inline table-callout-links"><div class="callout"><span>View this table:</span><ul class="callout-links"><li class="view-inline first"><a href="" class="table-expand-inline" data-table-url="/highwire/markup/4740569/expansion?postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed&amp;table-expand-inline=1" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View inline</a></li><li class="view-popup"><a href="/highwire/markup/4740569/expansion?width=1000&amp;height=500&amp;iframe=true&amp;postprocessors=highwire_tables%2Chighwire_reclass%2Chighwire_figures%2Chighwire_math%2Chighwire_inline_linked_media%2Chighwire_embed" class="colorbox colorbox-load table-expand-popup" rel="gallery-fragment-tables" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">View popup</a></li><li class="download-ppt last"><a href="/highwire/powerpoint/4740569" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-ppt" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download powerpoint</a></li></ul></div></div><div class="table-caption"><span class="table-label">Table 4:</span> <span class="caption-title">Architectural details for ST components.</span><p id="p-113" class="first-child"><em>G</em> denotes the number of genes (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-13-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-13">Eq. 13</a></strong>), <em>E</em> the dimension of cell embeddings (if used) (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-23-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-23">Eq. 22</a></strong>), <em>D</em><sub>pert</sub> the dimension of perturbation features (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-14-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-14">Eq. 14</a></strong>), <em>D</em><sub>batch</sub> the dimension of batch features (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-15-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-15">Eq. 15</a></strong>), and <em>h</em> the shared hidden dimension. All MLPs use GELU activation and Layer Normalization before activation, unless specified otherwise.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div></div><div id="sec-40" class="subsection"><h4>4.6.2. ST Training Details</h4><p id="p-114">All components are trained end-to-end with using the objectives described previously. These components include the control cell encoder <em>f</em><sub>cell</sub>, perturbation encoder <em>f</em><sub>pert</sub>, optional batch encoder <em>f</em><sub>batch</sub>, transformer backbone <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub>, reconstruction and decoding heads <em>f</em><sub>recon</sub> and <em>f</em><sub>decode</sub>.</p><p id="p-115">For the fine-tuning tasks (<strong><a id="xref-fig-3-10" class="xref-fig" href="#F3">Fig. 3G</a></strong>), we initialize a new ST model using the pretrained weights while selectively reinitializing specific components that are dataset-specific. In particular, the perturbation encoder <em>f</em><sub>pert</sub> is reinitialized to enable transfer across perturbation modalities. If ST is trained using cell embeddings, the gene decoder <em>f</em><sub>decode</sub> is also reinitialized to adapt to differences in gene coverage between datasets. Since different experimental platforms and datasets typically measure distinct subsets of genes, the decoder must be retrained to map from the shared embedding space to the target dataset’s specific gene expression space. All other components — <em>f</em><sub>cell</sub>, <em>f</em><sub>batch</sub>, and <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub> — retain their pretrained weights and are finetuned on the target dataset.</p></div><div id="sec-41" class="subsection"><h4>4.6.3. SE Hyperparameters</h4><p id="p-116">SE is a 600M parameter encoder-decoder model designed to learn cell representations by predicting gene expression variability. The encoder consists of 16 transformer layers, each with 16 attention heads and hidden dimension <em>h</em> = 2048. Each layer uses pre-normalization with a feed-forward network that expands to dimension 3 <em>× h</em>, and uses GELU activation. We apply dropout with probability 0.1 to both attention and feed-forward layers. The decoder is a multi-layer-perceptron (MLP), trained to recover gene expression given learned cell embeddings and target gene embeddings. We used the AdamW optimizer (<a id="xref-ref-65-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-65">Loshchilov and Hutter, 2017</a>) with a maximum learning rate of 10<sup>−5</sup>, weight decay of 0.01, and gradient clipping with zclip (<a id="xref-ref-56-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-56">Kumar et al., 2025</a>). The learning rate schedule consisted of linear warmup for the first 3% of total steps, followed by cosine annealing to 30% of the maximum learning rate. Before training, all SE weights are initialized sampling from Kaiming Uniform.</p></div><div id="sec-42" class="subsection"><h4>4.6.4. SE Training Details</h4><p id="p-117">SE was trained on a large-scale corpus of 14,420 AnnData files spanning 167 million human cells across the Arc scBaseCount (<a id="xref-ref-115-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-115">Youngblut et al., 2025</a>), CZ CELL×GENE (<a id="xref-ref-87-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-87">Program et al., 2025</a>), and Tahoe-100M (<a id="xref-ref-120-12" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-120">Zhang et al., 2025</a>) datasets for 4 epochs. To avoid data leakage, datasets were split into separate training and validation sets at the dataset level. To enable efficient training at scale, we utilize Flash Attention 2 (<a id="xref-ref-26-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-26">Dao, 2024</a>), with mixed precision (bf16) training (<a id="xref-ref-54-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-54">Kalamkar et al., 2019</a>). Training was distributed across 4 compute nodes, each with 8 NVIDIA H100 GPUs. The model was trained with an effective batch size of 3,072, using per-device batch size of 24 and gradient accumulation over 4 steps.</p></div></div><div id="sec-43" class="subsection"><h3>4.7. Evaluation</h3><p id="p-118">To comprehensively assess the ability of S<span class="sc">tate</span> to model perturbation effects, we designed an evaluation framework, Cell-Eval, that captures both expression-level accuracy and biologically meaningful patterns of differential expression. Cell-Eval measures not only statistical performance but also the model’s utility in simulating realistic Perturb-seq experiments.</p><div id="sec-44" class="subsection"><h4>4.7.1. Perturbation Evaluation Metrics</h4><p id="p-119">A key goal for perturbation models is to distinguish between different perturbation effects.</p><p id="p-120">Cell-Eval evaluates this using several complementary metrics:</p><div id="sec-45" class="subsection"><h5>Perturbation Discrimination Score</h5><p id="p-121">Adapted from <a id="xref-ref-113-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-113">Wu et al. (2024)</a>, this metric ranks predicted post-perturbation expression profiles by their similarity to ground truth. The score is defined as the normalized rank of the ground truth from the predicted perturbation with respect to all ground truth perturbations. It directly evaluates whether models can recover the relative differences between perturbations. In <a id="xref-ref-113-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-113">Wu et al. (2024)</a>, the rank ordering is computed using cosine similarity; we compute using Manhattan distances of transcriptomes due to two factors: larger sensitivity to magnitudes (<strong><a id="xref-fig-14-1" class="xref-fig" href="#F14">Fig. S10</a></strong>) and the lack of normalization in our loss (<a id="xref-ref-103-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-103">Steck et al., 2024</a>). Specifically, let <em>T</em> be the number of distinct perturbations, <em>y</em><sub><em>t</em></sub> the ground-truth profile for perturbation <em>t</em>, and <em>ŷ</em><sub><em>t</em></sub> its predicted profile. Using a distance <em>d</em>(<em>·, ·</em>) (here Manhattan or Euclidean), define
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-41"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-49.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-49.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
i.e. the number of other perturbations whose ground-truth profile is closer to <em>ŷ</em><sub><em>t</em></sub> than the correct one. The per-perturbation score is
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-42"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-50.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-50.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
ranging in [0, 1) with PDisc<sub><em>t</em></sub> = 0 indicating a perfect match (no closer profiles) and values near 1 signaling poor discrimination. The overall score is the mean
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-43"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-51.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-51.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
We report the normalized inverse perturbation discrimination score:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-44"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-52.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-52.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
so that a random predictor receives a score of 0.0, and a perfect predictor receives a score of 1.0.</p></div><div id="sec-46" class="subsection"><h5>Pearson Delta Correlation</h5><p id="p-122">This metric calculates a Pearson correlation coefficient between the predicted and observed expression deltas. The expression delta is computed as the difference in transcriptomes between a perturbed pseudobulk and an unperturbed pseudobulk. Specifically, for each perturbation <em>t</em> we form a pseudobulk <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-52"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-52.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-52.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and a control <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-53"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-53.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-53.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and compute the expression delta: <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-54"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-54.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-54.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, the element-wise absolute difference between the mean expression of perturbed cells <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-55"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-55.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-55.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and control cells <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-56"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-56.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-56.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. The evaluation score is the Pearson correlation
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-45"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-53.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-53.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
computed with <kbd>scipy.stats.pearsonr</kbd> across all perturbations.</p></div><div id="sec-47" class="subsection"><h5>Adjusted Mutual Information (AMI)</h5><p id="p-123">AMI is used to assess how well the model preserves the clustering structure of perturbations in the learned embedding space. We compute AMI by first aggregating cells by perturbation, then computing centroids for each perturbation by averaging the embeddings of all associated cells in both the real and predicted datasets. We then perform Leiden clustering on these centroids and compare the predicted cluster labels to the true cluster labels using AMI. The maximum AMI score across all resolutions is reported as the final metric, reflecting the degree to which the model’s predicted perturbation space captures the global structure of biological perturbations. Where ℛ is the set of Leiden clustering resolutions, and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-57"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-57.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-57.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-58"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-58.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-58.gif"/></noscript></span></span> are the clusterings of real and predicted centroids at resolution <em>r</em>,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-46"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-54.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-54.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-48" class="subsection"><h5>Mean Absolute Error (MAE) &amp; Mean Squared Error (MSE)</h5><p id="p-124">To assess preservation of shifts we calculate the MAE and MSE of each perturbation between the predicted and ground truth datasets. The MAE/MSE metrics are evaluated over predicted and observed pseudobulks. Formally, for perturbation <em>t</em>, let <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-59"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-59.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-59.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and <em>p</em><sub><em>t</em></sub> denote the predicted and observed pseudobulked expressions, respectively. Then,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-47"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-55.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-55.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div></div><div id="sec-49" class="subsection"><h4>4.7.2. Differential Expression</h4><p id="p-125">To evaluate biological relevance, Cell-Eval performs a differential expression analysis using the Wilcoxon rank-sum test and adjusts for multiple hypotheses using the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure, applied to both observed values and model predictions in test cell lines.</p><div id="sec-50" class="subsection"><h5>Notation</h5><p id="p-126">Fix a perturbation <em>t ∈</em> [<em>T</em>] = {1, …, <em>T</em>} and let <em>G</em> be the set of all genes. Define <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-60"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-60.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-60.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-61"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-61.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-61.gif"/></noscript></span></span> as the top-<em>k</em> significant DE genes (<em>p</em><sub>adj</sub> <em>&lt;</em> 0.05) in ground truth and predictions, ranked by log-fold changes <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-62"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-62.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-62.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-63"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-63.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-63.gif"/></noscript></span></span> as the complete significant sets. All rank correlations are Spearman’s, denoted by <em>ρ</em><sub>rank</sub>.</p><p id="p-127">C<span class="sc">ell</span>-E<span class="sc">val</span> also evaluates model performance using many complementary metrics:</p></div><div id="sec-51" class="subsection"><h5>DE Overlap Accuracy</h5><p id="p-128">For each perturbation, we identify the top <em>k</em> differentially expressed genes (<em>k</em> = 50, 100, 200, <em>N</em>), filtered by adjusted <em>p</em>-value and ranked by absolute log fold change. We compute the intersection between the predicted and true DEG sets and report the overlap as a fraction of <em>k</em>. When <em>k</em> = <em>N</em> the <em>k</em> value varies by perturbation where <em>N</em> is the total number of differentially expressed genes in the true DEG set. When <em>k</em> is not specified, we are referring to DE Overlap at <em>N</em>. That is,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-48"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-56.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-56.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-52" class="subsection"><h5>Top-<em>k</em> precision</h5><p id="p-129">For each perturbation, we compute how many of the top <em>k</em> DEGs from the ground truth appear in the model’s top <em>k</em> predicted DEGs, measuring precision at various thresholds. That is,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-49"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-57.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-57.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-53" class="subsection"><h5>Directionality Agreement</h5><p id="p-130">For each perturbation, we identify the set of significantly differentially expressed (DE) genes in the ground truth using an adjusted p-value threshold (e.g., <em>p &lt;</em> 0.05). We then find the intersection between the predicted and observed DE gene sets. For each overlapping gene, we check whether the predicted and true fold changes have the same direction. The directionality agreement is defined as the fraction of overlapping genes for which the predicted and observed directions match. Formally, where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-64"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-64.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-64.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and where log-fold changes Δ<sub><em>tg</em></sub> (true), <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-65"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-65.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-65.gif"/></noscript></span></span> (predicted). That is,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-50"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-58.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-58.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-54" class="subsection"><h5>Spearman Correlation</h5><p id="p-131">We compute the Spearman rank correlation between predicted and observed fold changes, restricted to genes that are significantly DE in ground truth. For each perturbation, we extract the list of significant genes (based on the adjusted p-value threshold) and compute the Spearman correlation coefficient on the log fold-changes between predicted and ground truth. That is,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-51"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-59.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-59.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-55" class="subsection"><h5>ROC-AUC</h5><p id="p-132">To assess the model’s ability to distinguish significant from non-significant DE genes, we assign binary labels to each gene in the observed data (1 if <em>p &lt;</em> 0.05, 0 otherwise) and use predicted − log<sub>10</sub> (adjusted p-values) as confidence scores. We compute the true positive rate (TPR) and false positive rate (FPR) at multiple thresholds and calculate the area under the ROC curve using auc. This metric evaluates the model’s ability to separate significant from nonsignificant genes regardless of the classification threshold. That is,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-52"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-60.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-60.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-56" class="subsection"><h5>PR-AUC</h5><p id="p-133">To measure the precision recall tradeoff of the model in identifying significant DE genes, as in ROC-AUC, we use binary labels from the observed and predicted − log<sub>10</sub>(p-values) as scores. This metric reflects the model’s ability to recover true positives with high precision when significant DE genes are sparse. That is,
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-53"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-61.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-61.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><div id="sec-57" class="subsection"><h5>Effect sizes</h5><p id="p-134">To compare the relative effect sizes of perturbations we calculate Spearman correlation coefficients for each perturbation on the number of differentially expressed genes (adjusted p-value <em>&lt;</em> 0.05) between predicted and ground truth. This lets us assess whether models accurately capture relative effect sizes. Formally, for set sizes <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-66"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-66.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-66.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, we compute
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-54"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-62.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-62.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div></div><div id="sec-58" class="subsection"><h4>4.7.3. Cell Embedding Evaluation Metrics</h4><p id="p-135">We also evaluated cell embeddings using both intrinsic and extrinsic metrics (<strong><a id="xref-fig-3-11" class="xref-fig" href="#F3">Fig. 3D</a></strong>).</p><div id="sec-59" class="subsection"><h5>Intrinsic Evaluation</h5><p id="p-136">To assess the extent to which embeddings capture perturbation-specific information, we trained a multilayer perceptron (MLP) with one hidden layer to classify perturbation labels from cell embeddings, measuring AUROC and accuracy. This classification task was performed separately for each cell line, with data partitioned such that 20% of cells from each perturbation group were randomly assigned to the test set and the remaining 80% to the training set. The MLP was trained using a cross-entropy loss. High classification performance indicates that embeddings maintain distinct representations of perturbation-induced states.</p></div><div id="sec-60" class="subsection"><h5>Extrinsic Evaluation</h5><p id="p-137">To evaluate the downstream utility of various cell embeddings (<strong><a id="xref-fig-3-12" class="xref-fig" href="#F3">Fig. 3E</a></strong>), we compared the performance of ST when trained with different embedding spaces. This approach captures the idea that embeddings rich in cellular information may not always be optimal for modeling perturbation effects. The ideal embedding space should balance both: it should preserve perturbation-specific information while supporting accurate prediction of perturbation-induced transitions across cell states.</p></div></div><div id="sec-61" class="subsection"><h4>4.7.4. Other Evaluations</h4><div id="sec-62" class="subsection"><h5>Drug Similarity Analysis</h5><p id="p-138">To evaluate how well different models preserved the relational structure between drug perturbations, we first constructed a representation of each drug as a vector of log<sub>2</sub>-fold changes in gene expression across cell lines. These vectors were aligned to a shared gene <em>×</em> cell line space. Pairwise drug-drug similarities were then calculated using cosine similarity. To assess the structural preservation of perturbations, we applied hierarchical clustering with average linkage to both the real and predicted distance matrices and computed the Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) between the resulting cluster assignments (using <em>k</em> = 5 clusters).</p></div><div id="sec-63" class="subsection"><h5>Cell Set Scaling</h5><p id="p-139">To evaluate the impact of cell set size in S<span class="sc">tate</span>, we ablated the number of cells per set and the batch size, such that the batch size times the cell set size was always 16,384, and measured the validation loss as a function of floating-point operations (FLOPs) (<strong><a id="xref-fig-1-7" class="xref-fig" href="#F1">Fig. 1C</a></strong>). These models were also compared to a pseudobulk model that replaces the self-attention in S<span class="sc">tate</span> with mean pooling, and masked attention version of S<span class="sc">tate</span>, where each cell can only attend to itself. As the cell set size increased, the validation loss improved significantly on held out cell lines, up to an optimal value of 256.</p></div><div id="sec-64" class="subsection"><h5>Survival Prediction Analysis</h5><p id="p-140">In order to assess whether the model could be used to predict the survival of each cell line within each drug well, a previously used measure of fitness (<a id="xref-ref-116-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-116">Yu et al., 2024</a>), we built a model to predict survival based on the mean expression of the 2K HVG set relative to DMSO for each cell type. Specifically, for the held-out set of cell lines and perturbations, we calculated a vector of true gene expression differences between DMSO and the perturbation. Then, we trained a support vector machine model to predict survival values based on these vectors, with all 3 doses from 35 randomly selected drugs held out. Finally, we used the same SVM model to predict the survival for these held-out treatments, first predicting on the true gene expression counts, followed by the predicted, cell-type mean, and pert-mean. In order to calculate confidence intervals, this process was repeated 10 times, with a different set of drugs being held out each time.</p><p id="p-141">The SVM model was a default scikit-learn support vector regressor model, with an “rbf” kernel. Relative fitness for each condition was calculated as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-67"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-67.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-67.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, where DMSO is matched to be in the same plate as the treatment.</p></div></div><div id="sec-65" class="subsection"><h4>4.7.5. Baseline Models</h4><p id="p-142">We describe and formalize various baseline models used as comparisons to S<span class="sc">tate</span>.</p><div id="sec-66" class="subsection"><h5>Perturbation Mean Baseline</h5><p id="p-143">This baseline predicts a perturbed expression profile as the control mean for that cell context plus a global perturbation offset learned from the training data. For each cell type <em>c</em> and perturbation <em>p</em> we first compute cell-type–specific means
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-55"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-63.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-63.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where 𝒞<sub><em>c</em></sub> is the set of control cells of type <em>c</em> and 𝒫<sub><em>c,p</em></sub> the perturbed cells of type <em>c</em> receiving perturbation <em>p</em>. Their difference is the cell-type offset <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-68"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-68.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-68.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. Averaging across all cell types that contain <em>p</em> yields a global offset
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-56"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-64.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-64.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Given a test cell type <em>t</em> and a perturbation label <em>p</em> the model outputs
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-57"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-65.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-65.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Thus, controls are reproduced exactly, while every non-control perturbation receives the same global shift.</p></div><div id="sec-67" class="subsection"><h5>Context Mean Baseline</h5><p id="p-144">This baseline predicts a cell’s post-perturbation profile by returning the average perturbed expression of <em>cells of the same cell type</em> observed in the training set. For every cell type <em>c</em> we collect all training cells whose perturbation is not the control and form the pseudo-bulk mean
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-58"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-66.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-66.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
At inference time, for a test cell <em>i</em> with cell type <em>c</em><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> and perturbation label <em>p</em><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> we predict
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-59"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-67.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-67.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
i.e. controls are passed through unchanged, whereas perturbed cells inherit their cell-type mean.</p></div><div id="sec-68" class="subsection"><h5>Linear Baseline</h5><p id="p-145">This baseline treats a perturbation as a low-rank, gene-wide linear displacement that is added to each cell’s own control expression (<a id="xref-ref-1-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-1">Ahlmann-Eltze et al., 2024</a>). Let <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-69"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-69.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-69.gif"/></noscript></span></span> be a fixed gene-embedding matrix (e.g. pretrained protein feature vectors, one row per gene) and <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-70"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-70.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-70.gif"/></noscript></span></span> a fixed perturbation-embedding matrix (one row per perturbation, one-hot). From the training set we first build an “expression-change” pseudo-bulk
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-60"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-68.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-68.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
so <em>Y ∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>G×P</em></sup> stores, for every gene <em>g</em> and perturbation <em>p</em>, the average change relative to that cell’s matched control. The model seeks a low-rank map <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-71"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-71.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-71.gif"/></noscript></span></span> and a gene-wise bias <strong>b</strong> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup><em>G</em></sup> such that
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-61"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-69.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-69.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
We obtain <em>K</em> in a single shot by solving the ridge-regularized least-squares problem
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-62"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-70.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-70.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
whose closed-form solution is
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-63"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-71.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-71.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
No gradient-based optimization is required, as once <em>K</em> is computed the model is fixed. For a test cell <em>i</em> with its own control profile <strong>x</strong><sup>ctrl,(<em>i</em>)</sup> and perturbation label <em>p</em><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup> the prediction is
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-64"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-72.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-72.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-72"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-72.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-72.gif"/></noscript></span></span> denotes the row of <em>P</em> corresponding to perturbation <em>p</em><sup>(<em>i</em>)</sup>. Thus each prediction preserves the cell’s basal state exactly and adds a perturbation-specific, low-rank shift learned from the training cohort, with model capacity controlled solely by the embedding dimensions and the ridge parameter <em>λ</em>.</p></div><div id="sec-69" class="subsection"><h5>Deep Learning Baselines</h5><p id="p-146">We benchmark S<span class="sc">tate</span> against several baselines that leverage related deep learning architectures for predicting perturbation effects across diverse cell contexts. These include two autoencoder-based models, scVI (<a id="xref-ref-63-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-63">Lopez et al., 2018</a>) and CPA (<a id="xref-ref-67-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-67">Lotfollahi et al., 2023</a>), as well as the transformer-based scGPT model (<a id="xref-ref-22-8" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-22">Cui et al., 2024</a>). scVI models gene expression distributions while accounting for technical noise and batch effects. CPA learns a compositional latent space that captures the additive effects of perturbation, dosage, and cell type. scGPT leverages generative pretraining on over 33 million cells to support zero-shot generalization across tasks including perturbation prediction. All models are evaluated using the respective datasets and setup described in <strong><a id="xref-fig-2-18" class="xref-fig" href="#F2">Fig. 2B</a></strong>.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-70"><h2 class="">5. Analysis: State Transition as Optimal Transport</h2><p id="p-147">In this section, we analyze the theoretical capacity of State Transition (ST) in learning optimal transport mappings across cellular distributions. First, we prove that the solution family of ST covers the optimal transport map between control and perturbed cell distributions. Second, we derive explicit constraints on ST for learning the unique continuous optimal transport map. Finally, we discuss the implications of the theoretical analysis and possible future directions.</p><div id="sec-71" class="subsection"><h3>5.1. ST Asymptotic Behavior and Solution Family</h3><p id="p-148">Optimal Transport (OT) provides a framework for comparing and aligning probability distributions by finding a mapping or coupling that minimizes a certain fixed cost. Traditional OT methods explicitly define a cost function and solve a linear program or iterative algorithm to find an optimal coupling or map (<a id="xref-ref-23-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-23">Cuturi, 2013</a>; <a id="xref-ref-85-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-85">Peyré et al., 2019</a>). The advent of deep learning has given rise to Neural Optimal Transport (Neural OT), which leverages neural networks to parameterize and solve OT problems (<a id="xref-ref-15-3" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-15">Bunne et al., 2024b</a>, 2023).</p><p id="p-149">Neural OT methods (<a id="xref-ref-15-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-15">Bunne et al., 2024b</a>) typically operate by either: (1) Parameterizing the OT map <em>T</em> directly using a neural network designed to enforce OT properties; (2) Parameterizing the OT coupling matrix (<strong>P</strong>) or its dual potentials (e.g., <em>α, β</em>) with neural networks; (3) Explicitly minimizing an OT cost function (or its dual objective) as the primary loss. For instance, CellOT (<a id="xref-ref-13-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-13">Bunne et al., 2023</a>) explicitly parameterizes the convex potentials of the dual optimal transport problem using Input Convex Neural Networks (ICNNs) to learn an optimal transport map <em>T</em><sub><em>k</em></sub> = <em>∇g</em><sub><em>k</em></sub> for each perturbation <em>k</em>, with <em>g</em><sub><em>k</em></sub> one of the dual potentials.</p><p id="p-150">ST, while not explicitly solving an OT problem in the traditional sense, performs a task related to Neural OT: learning a transformation that aligns unperturbed and perturbed cell distributions. This transformation is learned, not explicitly engineered for a fixed cost. However, motivated by rigorous theoretical work that demonstrates how transformers with fixed parameters can provably solve optimal transport by implementing gradient descent through engineered prompts (<a id="xref-ref-24-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-24">Daneshmand, 2024</a>), we demonstrate that, in an asymptotic setting, the solution family of our trained and optimized ST contains the unique continuous optimal transport map between cellular distributions when regularity assumptions on the distributions are met.</p><p id="p-151">For this analysis, we focus on a single training mini-batch, denoted as the <em>b</em>-th batch. We start by describing the mathematical formulation of ST, which aims to learn the effect of one single perturbation from cell population <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-73"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-73.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-73.gif"/></noscript></span></span> to <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-74"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-74.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-74.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. Recall that the input of the transformer for this batch is constructed by summing the control cell embeddings with the broadcasted perturbation and batch embeddings, <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-75"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-75.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-75.gif"/></noscript></span></span> (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-16-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-16">Eq. 16</a></strong>). The output of the transformer is computed as <strong>O</strong><sup>(<em>b</em>)</sup> = <strong>H</strong><sup>(<em>b</em>)</sup> + <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub><strong>H</strong><sup>(<em>b</em>)</sup> <em>∈</em> ℝ<sup>S×d</sup>, where <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub> is the transformer encoder (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-17-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-17">Eq. 17</a></strong>). We consider <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub> with <em>L</em> layers, where the input to the first layer is <strong>H</strong><sup>0</sup> = <strong>H</strong><sup>(<em>b</em>)</sup>:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-65"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-73.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-73.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Here <em>A</em><sup><em>i</em></sup> denotes the multi-head attention, <em>V</em> <sup><em>i</em></sup> denotes the attention value encoder, and <em>G</em> + Id denotes the feedforward layer combined with the residual connection. The layer normalization function is absorbed in each constructed function. Finally, for the <em>b</em>-th batch, a cell-wise linear transform defines the final output <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-76"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-76.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-76.gif"/></noscript></span></span> for the <em>S</em> cells (<strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-18-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-18">Eq. 18</a></strong>). We can equivalently rewrite this for each cell <em>s</em> as:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-66"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-74.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-74.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-77"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-77.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-77.gif"/></noscript></span></span> denotes the <em>s</em>-th row of <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-78"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-78.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-78.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, containing the predicted state of cell <em>s</em>, obtained by its embedding <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-79"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img
