<strong>O</strong><sup>(<em>b</em>)</sup>.</p><p id="p-152">The constants<span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-80"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-80.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-80.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, as well as the transforms <em>f</em><sub>cell</sub>, <em>f</em><sub>recon</sub> can be absorbed in the first and last layers of <em>f</em><sub>ST</sub>, which we therefore define as <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-81"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-81.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-81.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. Therefore, we can equivalently rewrite the predicted state of cell <em>s</em> as:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-67"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-75.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-75.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
where [<em>·</em>]<sub><em>s</em></sub> indicates that we access the <em>s</em>-th row of the obtained output. To simplify our analysis, we focus on the asymptotic setting where the cell set size <em>S</em> tend to infinity. Recall that each cell is sampled from the distribution 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub>. Then, as the cell number <em>S → ∞</em> with certain regularity conditions, the output for a single cell <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-82"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-82.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-82.gif"/></noscript></span></span> depends solely on <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-83"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-83.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-83.gif"/></noscript></span></span> itself and the overall distribution 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub>, because the attention mechanism effectively processes information from the entire distribution. Thus, the limiting operator can be defined per cell as:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-68"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-76.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-76.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
More detailed treatments are provided in recent works considering the limiting mean-field dynamics of transformers (<a id="xref-ref-38-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-38">Furuya et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-7-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-7">Biswal et al., 2024</a>). Notably, <strong><a id="xref-disp-formula-68-1" class="xref-disp-formula" href="#disp-formula-68">Eq. 56</a></strong> holds for an arbitrary cell <em>s</em> sampled from the distribution 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub>. The operator <strong>F</strong><sub>pert,batch</sub> defines a mapping from 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub> to <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-84"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-84.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-84.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. We further assume that the transformer model is expressive enough, such that minimizing the empirical kernel MMD objective is possible: <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-85"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-85.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-85.gif"/></noscript></span></span> with energy kernel <em>k</em>(<em>u, v</em>) = − <em>∥u</em> − <em>v∥</em> <sub>2</sub>. Our following lemma shows that zero empirical MMD indicates distributional matching with probability 1.</p><div id="statement-1" class="statement"><span class="statement-label">Lemma 1</span><p id="p-153">(Distributional matching via Empirical MMD). <em>Suppose that the supports of</em> <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-86"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-86.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-86.gif"/></noscript></span></span> <em>are bounded. When the cell number S → ∞, if the empirical MMD reaches zero, that is</em>, <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-87"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-87.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-87.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, <em>then with probability 1, we have</em>
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-69"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-77.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-77.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
<em>Reversely, if</em> <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-88"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-88.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-88.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, <em>then with probability 1, we have</em> <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-89"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-89.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-89.gif"/></noscript></span></span>.</p><p id="p-154"><em>Proof</em>. The empirical MMD used as the ST objective is defined as
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-70"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-78.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-78.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
</p></div><p id="p-155">Here we use <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-90"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-90.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-90.gif"/></noscript></span></span> to denote cells in <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-91"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-91.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-91.gif"/></noscript></span></span> respectively. The corresponding theoretical MMD on distributions <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-92"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-92.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-92.gif"/></noscript></span></span> is defined as
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-71"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-79.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-79.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
The empirical MMD is a V-statistic (<a id="xref-ref-39-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-39">Gretton et al., 2012</a>), thus when the support sets are bounded (thus the kernel value <em>k</em> is also bounded), by the result of SLLN for V-statistics (<a id="xref-ref-47-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-47">Hoeffding, 1961</a>; <a id="xref-ref-28-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-28">De la Pena and Giné, 2012</a>), we have
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-72"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-80.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-80.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
With the energy kernel <em>k</em>(<em>x, y</em>) = −<em>∥x</em> − <em>y∥</em> <sub>2</sub>, the theoretical MMD coincides with the energy distance <em>D</em><sup>2</sup> between distributions:
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-73"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-81.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-81.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Finally, zero energy distance implies equal distributions and vice versa (<a id="xref-ref-92-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-92">Rizzo and Székely, 2016</a>). □</p><p id="p-156">We define the continuous solution families that achieves zero empirical MMD and distributional matching as
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-74"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-82.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-82.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
Then, by <a id="xref-statement-1-1" class="xref-statement" href="#statement-1">Lemma 1</a>, we have
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-75"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-83.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-83.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
To simplify our analysis, we assume that our model is expressive enough to learn any element in <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-93"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-93.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-93.gif"/></noscript></span></span>, which is identical to ℱ with probability 1. We next demonstrate that, under mild regularity conditions, the unique optimal transport mapping from 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub> to 𝒟<sub>pert</sub> is within the solution family <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-94"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-94.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-94.gif"/></noscript></span></span>.</p><div id="statement-2" class="statement"><span class="statement-label">Theorem 2</span><p id="p-157">(Optimal Transport Mapping within the Solution Family of S<span class="sc">tate</span>). <em>Assume the densities of</em> 𝒟<sub><em>ctrl</em></sub>, 𝒟<sub><em>pert</em></sub> <em>are absolute continuous and bounded, and the support sets are strictly convex and compact with ℂ</em> <sup>2</sup> <em>boundary. Then the continuous optimal transport map T from</em> 𝒟<sub><em>ctrl</em></sub> <em>to</em> 𝒟<sub><em>pert</em></sub> <em>associated with the squared distance cost c</em>(<em>x, y</em>) = <em>∥x</em> − <em>y∥</em><sup>2</sup> <em>satisfies</em> <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-95"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-95.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-95.gif"/></noscript></span></span> <em>with probability 1</em>.</p><p id="p-158"><em>Proof</em>. Under the regularity conditions posed for distributions 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub>, 𝒟<sub>pert</sub>, applying Caffarelli’s theorem (<a id="xref-ref-16-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-16">Caffarelli, 1992</a>) shows that there exists an continuous and differentiable optimal transport map <em>T ∈</em> ℂ<sup>1</sup> between 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub>, 𝒟<sub>pert</sub>. Thus by definition, <em>T ∈</em> ℱ. Furthermore by <a id="xref-statement-1-2" class="xref-statement" href="#statement-1">Lemma 1</a>, <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-96"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-96.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-96.gif"/></noscript></span></span> with probability 1, in which case we also have <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-97"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-97.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-97.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. □</p></div><p id="p-159">While the theorem shows the existence of the continuous optimal transport map <em>T</em> and that it is covered by the solution family <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-98"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-98.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-98.gif"/></noscript></span></span> contains infinite additional elements beyond <em>T</em>, thus the optimal transport solution is not guaranteed to be learned by the model. This inherent flexibility is advantageous for S<span class="sc">tate</span>, as biological transformations often involve complexities and unmodeled variations that may not align perfectly with a single, fixed optimal transport solution. Nevertheless, in the next theorem, we show that if we impose constraints on the Jacobian in the ST model objective, then the optimal transport mapping is guaranteed to be learned:</p><div id="statement-3" class="statement"><span class="statement-label">Theorem 3</span><p id="p-160">(Constrained ST Model for Unique OT Map). <em>Under the same assumptions as <a id="xref-statement-2-2" class="xref-statement" href="#statement-2">Theorem 2</a>, consider the constrained solution family:</em>
<span class="disp-formula" id="disp-formula-76"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-84.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/graphic-84.gif"/></noscript></span>

</span>
<em>Then</em> <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-99"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-99.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-99.gif"/></noscript></span></span> <em>uniquely contains the continuous OT mapping T with probability 1:</em> <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-100"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-100.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-100.gif"/></noscript></span></span>.</p><p id="p-161"><em>Proof</em>. By Breiner’s theorem (<a id="xref-ref-10-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-10">Brenier, 1991</a>), <em>T</em> is the unique continuous optimal transport mapping regarding the quadratic cost, if and only if it is the gradient of a convex function <em>ψ</em>: <em>T</em> = <em>∇ψ</em>. As we already have <em>T ∈</em> ℂ<sup>1</sup> by Caffarelli’s theorem, the condition is equivalent to symmetric and semi-positive definite Jacobian in <em>T</em>. Further by <a id="xref-statement-1-3" class="xref-statement" href="#statement-1">Lemma 1</a>, <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-101"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-101.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-101.gif"/></noscript></span></span> with probability 1, and the uniqueness of <em>T</em> implies <span class="inline-formula" id="inline-formula-102"><span class="highwire-responsive-lazyload"><img src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" class="highwire-embed lazyload" alt="Embedded Image" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-102.gif"/><noscript><img class="highwire-embed" alt="Embedded Image" src="https://www.biorxiv.org/sites/default/files/highwire/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/embed/inline-graphic-102.gif"/></noscript></span></span>. □</p></div><p id="p-162">Our theoretical analysis can be seen as a corollary of Caffarelli and Brenier’s theorems on continuous optimal transport maps with quadratic cost. In particular, the final theorem hints that, we can enforce the model to learn an optimal transport mapping from 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub> to 𝒟<sub>pert</sub>, through imposing adequate regularizations on the Jacobian of the mapping. More precise constraints on the Jacobian may require additional architecture designs that explicitly achieve Jacobian constraints, or directly retrieve gradients of convex neural networks (<a id="xref-ref-72-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-72">Makkuva et al., 2020</a>; <a id="xref-ref-13-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-13">Bunne et al., 2023</a>).</p><p id="p-163">Despite the large feasible solution set that contains numerous overfitting solutions, neural network models have shown remarkable generalization capacities. An important aspect on neural network generalization capabilities is implicit bias (<a id="xref-ref-101-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-101">Soudry et al., 2018</a>; <a id="xref-ref-40-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-40">Gunasekar et al., 2018</a>; <a id="xref-ref-107-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-107">Vardi, 2023</a>), which means gradient descent favor certain solutions, for instance, those minimally shifted compared with initial weights (<a id="xref-ref-40-2" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-40">Gunasekar et al., 2018</a>). Therefore we hypothesize that the optimization of ST may lead to solutions with smallest “overall shifts” from 𝒟<sub>ctrl</sub> to 𝒟<sub>pert</sub>, thus resembling a optimal transport mapping with some unknown cost function. Theoretical characterization of the implicit bias in the transition model is out of the scope here and remains promising future research.</p></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-72"><h2 class="">6. Exploring the Latent Space of S<span class="sc">tate</span></h2><p id="p-164">Initial interpretability analyses reveal that S<span class="sc">tate</span>’s predictions can be better understood through its internal computation patterns, particularly suggesting that the transition model architecture harnesses heterogeneity to enhance perturbation prediction.</p><div id="sec-73" class="subsection"><h3>State embeddings display markers of biological knowledge learned from self-supervision</h3><p id="p-165">We begin by evaluating cell embeddings produced by SE. Given the embedding model’s impressive performance in a zero-shot setting across datasets, it is instructive to examine S<span class="sc">tate</span>’s embedding structure. As shown in <strong><a id="xref-fig-13-1" class="xref-fig" href="#F13">Fig. S9A</a></strong>, the principal components of State embeddings capture nuanced distinctions across and within cell types, suggesting a rich representation for transfer. Notably, embeddings for K-562 and Jurkat (two leukemia cell lines) cluster closely in this space, implying similarity under S<span class="sc">tate</span>’s learned representation, following results in <a id="xref-ref-75-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-75">Nadig et al. (2025)</a>. Importantly, SE was trained without direct knowledge that K-562 and Jurkat share these similarities, and recognizes and leverages such biological concepts purely from self-supervision over gene expression data.</p></div><div id="sec-74" class="subsection"><h3>The State transition model leverages cell heterogeneity through self-attention</h3><p id="p-166">Early efforts to reverse-engineer deep machine learning models have commonly focused on visualizing activations, revealing how neural networks encode concepts (<a id="xref-ref-118-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-118">Zeiler and Fergus, 2014</a>; <a id="xref-ref-79-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-79">Olah et al., 2017</a>; <a id="xref-ref-4-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-4">Bau et al., 2017</a>; <a id="xref-ref-21-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-21">Clark et al., 2019</a>). Similarly to these works, since all inputs to ST originate from the same cell line, we aim to unpack the model’s inner workings with respect to cell heterogeneity. To accomplish this, norm-based contribution magnitudes and self-attention maps for well-defined cell sets can discover general behaviors in the model’s methods for prediction. Running inference with the Replogle-Nadig dataset, we first find that the largest changes to hidden state happen at the final layer of the transition model, across all attention heads (<strong><a id="xref-fig-13-2" class="xref-fig" href="#F13">Fig. S9B</a></strong>). A deeper dive into these heads reveals intriguing structural behaviors. We find that the attention heads on the final layer take on extremely different roles. Specifically, we identify major two classes of heads:</p><ol class="list-ord " id="list-5"><li id="list-item-12"><p id="p-167"><strong>Heterogeneity-Sensitive heads</strong>, which exhibit scattered, high-magnitude Query-Key interactions across many cells or only focus on the corresponding Key matrix for the Query (<strong><a id="xref-fig-13-3" class="xref-fig" href="#F13">Fig. S9C</a>&amp;D</strong>).</p></li><li id="list-item-13"><p id="p-168"><strong>Heterogeneity-Insensitive heads</strong>, which fixate on either narrow Key matrices, often from just a few cells, and broadly apply across all Queries (<strong><a id="xref-fig-13-4" class="xref-fig" href="#F13">Fig. S9C&amp;D</a></strong>). These behaviors mimic operations akin to pseudobulking (i.e. averaging across single-cell profiles to mimic bulk measurements) or PerturbMean-style computations, two robust but less expressive baselines to S<span class="sc">tate</span>.</p></li></ol><p id="p-169">To further dissect heterogeneity-sensitive attention behaviors, we construct an inference-time example with a mixed cell set composed of 50% K-562 and 50% Jurkat cells (<strong><a id="xref-fig-13-5" class="xref-fig" href="#F13">Fig. S9D</a></strong>). Upon inputting this hybrid set, we observe that certain attention heads adaptively shift their behavior: heterogeneity-aware heads become more polarized, and a novel insensitive head emerges, one that selectively utilizes only the Key matrices from one cell type while computing over Queries from both. This asymmetric attention pattern reveals that S<span class="sc">tate</span> is not merely averaging across cell sets, but actively discriminating between them in a context-sensitive fashion. Such computations suggest that the transition model internalizes representations of heterogeneity and dynamically leverages them during inference for improved prediction performance.</p><p id="p-170">These initial results underscore the interplay of leveraging transformer interpretability to better understand perturbation effects in cells. Future work can apply more sophisticated interpretability methods—beyond attention maps, which have shown limited utility for exact localization (<a id="xref-ref-42-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-42">Hase et al., 2023</a>; <a id="xref-ref-17-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-17">Chefer et al., 2021</a>)—to better understand S<span class="sc">tate</span>’s prediction mechanisms, building on approaches that optimize for extracting sparse, finer-grained model features (<a id="xref-ref-11-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-11">Bricken et al., 2023</a>; <a id="xref-ref-6-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-6">Bhaskar et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-33-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-33">Dunefsky et al., 2024</a>; <a id="xref-ref-2-1" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-2">Ameisen et al., 2025</a>).</p></div></div><div class="section" id="sec-75"><h2 class="">Code and model availability</h2><p id="p-171">All code for this project is available at <a href="https://github.com/ArcInstitute/State">https://github.com/ArcInstitute/State</a>, <a href="https://github.com/ArcInstitute/Cell-load">https://github.com/ArcInstitute/Cell-load</a>, <a href="https://github.com/ArcInstitute/Cell-eval">https://github.com/ArcInstitute/Cell-eval</a>, <a href="https://github.com/ArcInstitute/State-reproduce">https://github.com/ArcInstitute/State-reproduce</a>.</p><p id="p-172">Model parameters are available on Huggingface: <a href="https://huggingface.co/arcinstitute/SE-600M">https://huggingface.co/arcinstitute/SE-600M</a>, <a href="https://huggingface.co/arcinstitute/ST-Tahoe">https://huggingface.co/arcinstitute/ST-Tahoe</a>, <a href="https://huggingface.co/arcinstitute/ST-Parse">https://huggingface.co/arcinstitute/ST-Parse</a></p></div><div class="section" id="sec-76"><h2 class="">Author contributions</h2><p id="p-174">A.K.A and Y.H.R. conceived the S<span class="sc">tate</span> project. (B) Heatmaps comparing the true overall perturbation effects as measured by number of differentially expressed genes with the predicted perturbation effect sizes by State." src="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/F8.medium.gif" width="440" height="382"/></noscript></span></a></div></div><ul class="highwire-figure-links inline"><li class="download-fig first"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/F8.large.jpg?download=true" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-download" title="Download Figure S4:" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Download figure</a></li><li class="new-tab last"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/F8.large.jpg" class="highwire-figure-link highwire-figure-link-newtab" target="_blank" data-icon-position="" data-hide-link-title="0">Open in new tab</a></li></ul></div><div class="fig-caption"><span class="fig-label">Figure S4:</span> <span class="caption-title">Additional metrics for evaluating identification of significant genes.</span><p id="p-179" class="first-child">Models were trained and evaluated on chemical, signaling and genetic perturbation datasets (<a id="xref-ref-120-13" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-120">Zhang et al., 2025</a>; <a id="xref-ref-90-9" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-90">Replogle et al., 2022</a>; <a id="xref-ref-75-7" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-75">Nadig et al., 2025</a>). Comparisons included the mean baselines from (A), a simple linear model (<a id="xref-ref-1-4" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-1">Ahlmann-Eltze et al., 2024</a>), autoencoder-based models (scVI (<a id="xref-ref-63-5" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-63">Lopez et al., 2018</a>), CPA (<a id="xref-ref-67-6" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-67">Lotfollahi et al., 2023</a>)), and a foundation model (scGPT (<a id="xref-ref-22-9" class="xref-bibr" href="#ref-22">Cui et al., 2024</a>)). Performance was assessed using standard Perturb-Seq outputs: expression counts and differentially expressed (DE) genes, with the following metrics considered: <strong>(A)</strong> Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC), summarizing model performance in identifying true DE genes, especially under class imbalance. <strong>(B)</strong> Heatmaps comparing the true overall perturbation effects as measured by number of differentially expressed genes with the predicted perturbation effect sizes by S<span class="sc">tate</span>.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div><div id="F9" class="fig pos-float type-figure  odd"><div class="highwire-figure"><div class="fig-inline-img-wrapper"><div class="fig-inline-img"><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/F9.large.jpg?width=800&amp;height=600&amp;carousel=1" title="Additional metrics for evaluating top DE gene prediction. (B) The final layer in ST contributes largely to the changing hidden state, across all attention heads. (C) Visualizing the attention heads (Query &#xD7; Key) for the final layer displays striking variation across attention heads. (D) Constructing an inference-time example with a mixed cell set of K-562 and Jurkat visually displays how self-attention can model heterogeneity across the cells in the input set." src="data:image/gif;base64,R0lGODlhAQABAIAAAAAAAP///yH5BAEAAAAALAAAAAABAAEAAAIBRAA7" data-src="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/F13.medium.gif" width="440" height="339"/><noscript><img class="highwire-fragment fragment-image" alt="Initial interpretability analyses indicate how State leverages learnt biological information to make it&#x2019;s predictions. (A) SE, without biological labels, learns that K-562 and Jurkat share similarities (both being from leukemia cell lines) just from gene expression data. (B) The final layer in ST contributes largely to the changing hidden state, across all attention heads. (C) Visualizing the attention heads (Query &#xD7; Key) for the final layer displays striking variation across attention heads. (D) Constructing an inference-time example with a mixed cell set of K-562 and Jurkat visually displays how self-attention can model heterogeneity across the cells in the input set." href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/biorxiv/early/2025/07/10/2025.06.26.661135/F14.large.jpg?width=800&amp;height=600&amp;carousel=1" title="Distributions of real gene expression data with different discrimination operations displays how cosine similarity skews ranking metrics. We find that cosine similarity, by not accounting for the magnitudes of genes in expression data, reflects excessive similarity between real gene expression values and predicted values, which causes a distribution shift in downstream rank based metrics. Comparably, Euclidean distances (L2) and Manhattan distances (L1) better separate varying gene expressions within groups of cells.</p><div class="sb-div caption-clear"></div></div></div></div><div class="section ack" id="ack-1"><h2 class="">Acknowledgments</h2><p id="p-173">We thank Joseph Caputo, Alden Woodrow, Julia Kazaks, Jingtian Zhou, Brian Hie, Tony Hua, Paul Datlinger, Nianzhen Li, Brian Yu, David Lara-Astiaso, Kristen Seim, Lorena Saavedra, Rachel Senturia, Scott Newins, Gwanggyu Sun, Tim Hudelmaier, Jason Swinderman, for helpful discussions and assistance with manuscript preparation.
